## Page 5


Abstract
We give a fully algebraic proof of an important theorem of Demailly, stating the existence of
many Green-Griﬃths jet diﬀerentials on a complex projective manifold of general type. To this end,
we introduce a new algebraic version of the Morse inequalities, which we use in our proof as an algebraic
counterpart to Demailly’s and Bonavero’s holomorphic Morse inequalities.
Our proof also applies in positive characteristic, giving the existence of Hasse-Schmidt jet diﬀer-
entials for a smooth projective variety of general type over an arbitrary algebraically closed ﬁeld.
2020 Mathematics Subject Classiﬁcation. Primary 14A15, 32Q45.
Key words and phrases. Jet diﬀerentials, Hasse-Schmidt diﬀerentials, complex hyperbolicity.
v


## Page 7


Introduction
0.1. Jet diﬀerentials and hyperbolicity
The Green-Griﬃths-Lang conjecture. Jet diﬀerential equations are certainly one of the most im-
portant tool in the study of complex hyperbolicity. Recall that a complex manifold X is said to be
Brody hyperbolic if it does not contain any entire curve, namely, any image of a non-constant holo-
morphic map f ∶C →X. In the projective setting, the tantalizing Green-Griﬃths-Lang conjecture
predicts that this property should essentially be equivalent to the algebro-geometric property of being
of general type :
Conjecture 1 (Green-Griﬃths [GG80], Lang [Lan87]). Let X be a complex projective manifold.
The following are equivalent :
(1) X is of general type ;
(2) there exists a proper algebraic subset Exc(X) ⊊X, such that X is Brody hyperbolic modulo
Exc(X) i.e. for any non constant holomorphic map f ∶C Ð→X, we have f(C) ⊆Exc(X).
Here, we say that a projective manifold X is of general type if its canonical line bundle KX is big,
i.e. if we have the maximal growth h0(X,K⊗m
X ) ≥C mdimX for some C > 0.
If C is a curve, then C is of general type if and only if g(C) ≥2, and in this case, the result
is a consequence of Riemann’s uniformization theorem. Indeed, any entire curve has to lift to the
universal covering ∆→C, and thus must be constant by Liouville’s theorem. In higher dimension, the
conjecture is still largely open.
The bundles of Green-Griﬃths jet diﬀerentials. The relevance of jet diﬀerential techniques for
the study of complex hyperbolicity problems has ﬁrst been evidenced by Green and Griﬃths [GG80].
For any integers k,m ≥1, these authors have constructed a vector bundle EGG
k,mΩX Ð→X, the so-
called bundle of Green-Griﬃths jet diﬀerentials, whose local sections represent holomorphic ordinary
diﬀerential equations of order k and degree m on X (acting on germs of holomorphic curves with values
in X). The main interest of these jet diﬀerentials in view of Conjecture 1 comes from the following
fundamental theorem.
Theorem 1 ([SY96, Dem97]). Let A be an ample line bundle on X.
Let k,m ∈N∗, and let
P ∈H0(X,EGG
k,mΩX ⊗O(−A)). Then, any non constant holomorphic map f ∶C Ð→X is a solution to
the diﬀerential equation P, i.e. P(f;f ′,...,f (k)) ≡0.
The theorem above is the ﬁrst step of a general strategy to ﬁnd restrictions on the geometry of
entire curves, in the hope of eventually proving the Green-Griﬃths-Lang conjecture. In many contexts,
this strategy has permitted to obtain strong hyperbolicity results e.g. for hypersurfaces of Pn+1 (see
e.g. [DMR10, B´er15, Bro17, Siu15, Den16, Dem18, BK24, Cad24], complements of hypersurfaces of Pn
(see [Dar15, BD19]), surfaces of general type (see [Bog77, McQ98, RR13])... The ﬁeld of application
of these jet diﬀerential techniques has also been recently extended to the orbifold setting by Campana,
Darondeau, Demailly and Rousseau in [CDR20, CDDR24].
The strategy sketched above will work if we are able to prove the existence of many global jet
diﬀerential equations on a given manifold of general type. The most general result in this direction is
due to Demailly [Dem11], and can be stated as follows.

In particular, if A is an ample line bundle on X, and if m ≫k ≫1, we have
H0(X,EGG
k,mΩX ⊗O(−A)) ≠0.
The proof of Theorem 2 given by Demailly in [Dem11] is fundamentally analytic in nature: it is
based on the holomorphic Morse inequalities he introduced in [Dem85], and that were later extended
to the singular setting by Bonavero [Bon98].
0.2. Main results
Our purpose in the current notes is twofold:
(1) give an fully algebraic (and hopefully, essentially self-contained) proof of Theorem 2 ;
(2) extend the ﬁeld of validity of Demailly’s result to positive characteristic.
Let us give some details.
Demailly’s proof. Holomorphic and algebraic Morse inequalities. Before explaining what needs to

hope to convert it to an algebraic setting.

The main candidate for an algebraic version of the holomorphic Morse inequalities are the algebraic

This general strategy has been followed by several authors (starting with Diverio [Div08, Div09],

some constant d(n) ∈N. Unfortunately, this algebraic method does not seem to give the bigness of

k,●ΩX when d ≥n + 3 and k ≫1, which would be the expected bound on d according to Theorem
2. There seems here to be a discrepancy between the results the algebraic and analytic methods can
provide, at least with this speciﬁc method. 2
A new version of the algebraic Morse inequalities. It seems to us the discrepancy discussed above
comes from a rather restrictive setting for the statement of the algebraic Morse inequalities. Rather
than dealing only with the case where L = A−B, with A,B nef (or even very ample), it would be much
more ﬂexible to be able to deal with any diﬀerence of eﬀective divisors A,B.
In the following discussion, we propose to follow this idea, and to give accordingly a new algebraic
version of the Morse inequalities.
In the case where A and B are very ample, the standard algebraic Morse inequalities can be
proved by induction: writing L = O(A −B), we can use an exact sequence argument to reduce the
problem to showing the inequalities for both L∣A on the lower dimensional variety A (resp. for L∣B
on B). Actually, assuming that A and B are merely eﬀective does not present much diﬃculty for this
argument. However, for the induction to work, one sees that we need to assume now that we also have
a decomposition on A of the form:
L∣A ≅OA(AA −BA),
with AA,BA eﬀective on A, and similarly for L∣B.
This remark quickly leads us to trying to state Morse inequalities in terms of stratiﬁcations on our
manifolds, in the following sense. Let L Ð→X be a line bundle over a complex manifold, and let e
be a trivialization of L over some open dense subset U ⊆X, where X ∖U is the support of a Cartier
divisor D such that L = O(D). We can now extract a particular combinatorial data of this situation:
(1) write D = D+ −D−, where D+, D−are eﬀective;
(2) note for later use the multiplicities of e along the irreducible components of D+ and D−;
(3) restrict L to D, and ﬁnd a trivialization of this restriction on a Zariski dense open subset of
D;
(4) repeat this operation with X replaced by each component of D.
Inductively, this deﬁnes a stratiﬁcation Σ on X, with the data of a trivialization e over Σ (see
Section 3.1 for the complete deﬁnitions). Then the data of Σ = (Σ,e) and of the multiplicities computed
along the way permits to deﬁne truncated Chern intersection numbers deg c1(X,Σ)n
[≤i], for all 0 ≤i ≤n.
The previous deﬁnitions actually make sense over an arbitrary ﬁeld; we may also replace L by a
Q-line bundle. In the end, this allows to formulate generalized Morse inequalities as follows.
Theorem 3 (= Theorem 3.3.5). Let X be a projective variety of dimension n over an arbitrary
ﬁeld

be another line bundle on X. Then, for each integer 0 ≤i ≤n, and any m divisible enough, we have

∑

The core of our proof is very similar in spirit to the one of Angelini [Ang96]: it is an induction on
dimX, using quite standard dimensional considerations on long exact sequences of coherent sheaves.
The main technical diﬃculty comes from the fact that the induction step requires several clean-up
operations to be able to make sense of the exact sequence argument:
(1) the fact that we deal with Q-line bundles requires us to take cyclic covers on X. If char(k) > 0,
this actually does not add many complications, but some care needs to be applied;
(2) the main technical diﬃculty comes from the fact that the irreducible components of X ∖U
above are not necessarily Cartier, so we need to perform a modiﬁcation on X to get back to
this situation. In characteristic 0, this could be easily achieved by Hironaka’s resolution of
singularities, but this is a bit more technical in positive characteristic: we need to blow-up
Weil divisors, and deal carefully with the exceptional components that may appear in the
process.

Suppose now that we are given a trivialized stratiﬁcation Σ = (Σ,e), adapted to KX = L1⊗...⊗Ln.

Arbitrary characteristic and Hasse-Schmidt diﬀerentials. The algebraicity of the proof discussed
above opens the door to proving corresponding existence results over an arbitrary ﬁeld. We believe
this might be relevant in particular for a future study of hyperbolicity in non-archimedean settings
(e.g. over local ﬁelds such as Fp((t)) or Qp).3
The bundles of Green-Griﬃths jet diﬀerential have a natural replacement in arbitrary character-
istic, namely the bundles of Hasse-Schmidt diﬀerentials (discussed in particular by Vojta in [Voj04]).
If X is a smooth variety over a ﬁeld
k, these vector bundles EHS
k,mΩX also have an interpretation in
terms of diﬀerential equations for inﬁnitesimal curves

0.3. Organization of the text
These notes are organized in ﬁve chapters. Three appendices contain several technical results or
some background used in the rest of the work.
Chapter 1. The main purpose of this chapter is to give an introductory presentation of the Hasse-
Schmidt jet diﬀerentials, aimed mainly at researchers with a more complex geometric background. We
do not claim a lot of originality for this part, which mostly consist in a sanity check that everything
relevant works as intended. In particular, we give a detailed presentation of the standard ﬁltration on
Hasse-Schmidt diﬀerentials, crucial in the rest of the text. We give also a very short presentation of the
possible use of these jet diﬀerentials to prove algebraic hyperbolicity results in positive characteristic.
Chapter 2. This chapter gives a recollection on weighted Segre classes, used by the author in [Cad20,
Cad24] to perform intersection computations in explicit cases.
Our basic idea is that Theorem 4
should be interpreted as a truncated version of a corresponding Riemann-Roch formula ; accordingly,
the integral in (0.3) can be thought of as a ”truncated Segre class”. The purpose of this chapter is
mainly to rederive a proof of the Whitney formula for truncated Segre classes (see Proposition 2.1.1)
with an argument making appear an integral over a simplex, exactly as in Theorem 4. Hopefully, this
will help the reader to see more closely the argument behind the proof of this latter result.
Chapter 3. This chapter is the heart of the text. After some introductory deﬁnitions, we give the proof
of our algebraic version of the Morse inequalities. The proof is performed in Section 3.3; as explained
above, it is an induction using several clean-up operations. In principle, it is self contained and uses
only elementary results of algebraic geometry, most of which were gathered in Annex A.
Chapter 4. This chapter presents the proof of Theorem 4 and several related results. As we explained
above, the idea is basically to follow the proof of Whitney formula discussed in Chapter 2, but replacing
the use of the Hirzebruch-Riemann-Roch theorem with the algebraic Morse inequalities.
There is a technical diﬃculty related to the fact that we work with Q-line bundles instead of
standard ones. when taking symmetric powers of a direct sum of line bundles, this has the consequence
that our vector bundles might be deﬁned only up to tensoring with a torsion line bundle; so it might
not be clear that the left hand side of (0.3) is well deﬁned if we replace the Li with Q-line bundles.
The remedy is to choose representatives for the image of Pic(X) →Pic(X)Q in a subgroup ﬁxed in
advance; this is discussed in Section 4.1.1.
Chapter 5. Finally, we give a proof of the main Theorem 5.0.1. By what we have explained above,
we are lead back to Theorem 4 after using ﬁrst a reduction to the graded object of EHS
k,mΩX, and then
a splitting principle for ΩX. Next, we have to produce a stratiﬁcation on a variety X′ admitting a
suitable generically ﬁnite morphism X′ →X; this is done using some of the results of Chapter 3.


## Page 13


ACKNOWLEDGMENTS.
xiii
The last step is to estimate the integral giving the leading term of the inequalities. For this, we
expand on Demailly’s Monte-Carlo interpretation, and show that this integral can be interpreted as
the expectancy value of a random variable drawn uniformly in a simplex. As k goes to inﬁnity, a sort
of ”law of large numbers” gives the asymptotic estimate appearing in the right hand side of (0.4).
Annex A. This annex gathers several algebraic geometry lemmas, used throughout the text. Most of
them are elementary in nature.
Annex B. We have included here several basic results related to volume computations of simplexes,
and random variables in these sets, for lack of a proper reference. These results are used in particular
in Chapter 2 and Chapter 5.
Annex C. This annex presents several basic results concerning stacks in positive characteristic, and in
particular the weighted projective spaces that appear as ﬁbers of the Green-Griﬃths jet spaces. The
results of this annex are quite classical and well known to experts; they are mostly intended to the
reader wondering about the consistency of the deﬁnitions, in particular in the positive characteristic
setting.

## Page 15


Notation and terminology
Scheme theory.
●We ﬁx an algebraically closed ﬁeld
k of arbitrary characteristic. In some parts of the text, we
will drop this assumption that
k is algebraically closed; we will always mention this explicitely.
●A scheme will be a separated scheme of ﬁnite type over
k. A proper scheme is a scheme
admitting a proper morphism to Spec
k. Unless otherwise mentioned, all ﬁber products will
be taken over Spec
k.
●A variety will be an irreducible and reduced scheme. A scheme is pure dimensional if all its
irreducible components are varieties of the same dimension.
●A scheme is normal if all its local rings are normal. This is equivalent to asking that all its
connected components are normal varieties.
●If X is a scheme, we will use the notation Zk(X) (resp. Zk(X)Q) to denote the cycle group
of dimension k cycles on X with coeﬃcients in Z (resp. Q). Similary Ak(X) (resp. Ak(X)Q)
will denote the Chow group of k-th dimensional cycles class with integral (resp. rational)
coeﬃcients. We refer to [Ful98] for more details.
●A Q-line bundle on a scheme X is an element of Pic(X) ⊗Z Q. We will sometimes qualify
elements of Pic(X) as standard line bundles, as opposed to Q-line bundles.
●If V,W are varieties, a dominant map f ∶V →W is birational if it is an isomorphism above
a neighborhood of the generic point of W.
●If V is a variety, a modiﬁcation of V is a proper birational morphism W Ð→V .
Trees.
●A rooted tree (or tree, for short) is a ﬁnite connected acyclic undirected graph, with a distin-
guished node called the root. We will sometimes speak of T as the set of its nodes, implying
the additional data formed by the edges.
●In a tree, a node a is the parent of an other node b, if it is the closest node on the unique path
connecting b to the root. In this case b is called a child of a. We say that b is an ancestor of
a if there is a path leading from a to the root passing through b. Conversely, we say that a
is a descendant of b.
●A node with no children is called a leaf.
●A root-to-leaf path is a path leading the root to any leaf. These paths are in bijection with
the leaves.
●An embedding of trees ι ∶T ↪T ′ is a injective map between the set of nodes, that preserves
the parent-child relation induced by the edges. We will say also that T embeds as a subtree
of T ′. If ι preserves the root, we say that it is a rooted embedding of trees.
●If T is a rooted tree and a ∈T is a node, then the subtree of T based at a is the subtree of T
formed by elements of T that have a as an ancestor. Its root identiﬁes with a.

CHAPTER 1
Green-Griﬃths and Hasse-Schmidt jet diﬀerentials
In this chapter,
k will be a ﬁeld of arbitrary characteristic, not necessarily algebraically closed. A
scheme will mean a separated scheme of ﬁnite type above Spec
k (see the section on notation).
1.1. Hasse-Schmidt diﬀerentials
It is a classical fact that diﬀerential calculus is better behaved in positive characteristic if one
accepts to work with divided diﬀerentials. When it comes to deﬁning the theory of jet diﬀerentials,
one way to proceed is to use the theory of Hasse-Schmidt higher derivations, as advocated by Vojta
[Voj04].
We refer to Vojta’s notes for an axiomatic presentation of the construction. In the following section,
we will present a more geometric approach, perhaps closer to its complex geometric equivalent: we
will ﬁrst describe these jet diﬀerentials on (open subsets) of aﬃne spaces, and then use ´etale charts to
deﬁne them locally and glue them on any given smooth scheme. Note that our discussion ultimately
relies in Vojta’s proofs; we hope it should be suﬃciently self contained nonetheless.
1.1.1. Main deﬁnitions. Let us recall the concept of (divided) higher derivations, instrumental in
everything that follows.

Remark 1.1.11. Just to ﬁx the ideas, let us show how to compute such diﬀerentials in the 2-
dimensional ring A =
k[x,y]. We have for example
D2(x2y) = (x(1))2y + 2xx(2)y + 2xx(1)y(1) + x2y(2).
This formula can be obtained by dividing by 2! the second derivative computed formally as follows
(compare e.g. with (1.1)):
(x2y)′′ = 2(x′)2y + 2xx′′y + 4xx′y′ + x2y′′.
Of course, this division operation does not make sense if char(k) = 2, and we must resort to the Leibniz
rule of Deﬁnition 1.1.1 to perform the computation in full generality.
The description in aﬃne coordinates can be used to provide trivializations in ´etale charts: this is
what we are now going to explain.

1.3. Jets and hyperbolicity
Let us discuss here very brieﬂy about the link between Hasse-Schmidt diﬀerentials and algebraic
hyperbolicity over an arbitrary ﬁeld. Again, the result are closely related to what is known in the
complex setting.
In this section, we assume for simplicity that
k is algebraically closed, even though this is not
strictly necessary.
Definition 1.3.1. Let X be a smooth projective variety, and let L be an ample line bundle over
X. We say that X is algebraically hyperbolic if there exists constants A,B > 0 such that for any smooth
projective curve C over
k, and any non-constant separable morphism f ∶C →X, we have
−χ(C) ≥AdegC(f ∗L) + B.
Similarly, we say that X is algebraically hyperbolic modulo Z ⊂X if the inequality holds for any f as
above as soon as f(C) /⊂Z.
Remark 1.3.2.
(1) This deﬁnition is the analogue of Demailly’s notion of algebraic hyper-
bolicity (see [Dem12, §3]). If the base ﬁeld is C, it is implied by Brody hyperbolicity (i.e. the
absence of non-constant holomorphic map f ∶C →X), and conjecturally equivalent to it.
(2) the assumption that f is separable in the above deﬁnition is required by the existence of the
Frobenius morphism: by considering a morphism of the form
C′ F m
Ð→C Ð→X,
we can get deg f ∗L arbitrarily large, while keeping χ(C′) = χ(C) constant.
Exactly as in the complex case, we can show in general that the existence of ”suﬃciently many”
jet diﬀerential equations implies the algebraic hyperbolicity of X. Let us give some details.
Proposition 1.3.3. Let X be projective variety endowed with an ample line bundle L. Let pk ∶
XGG
k
→X be the standard projection, and let
B+(Ok(1)) ∶= ⋂
m>0
Bs(Ok(m) ⊗p∗
kL−1).
be the augmented base locus of Ok(1). Let B ⊂B+(Ok(1)) be the union of the irreducible components
that are not included in the singular substack XGG,sing
k

## Page 35


CHAPTER 2
Weighted Segre classes and asymptotic Euler characteristics
The purpose of this chapter is twofold. First, we will explain how to present Green and Griﬃths’
classical asymptotic Euler computations [GG80], using weighted Segre classes. We will rederive an
elementary proof of the Whitney formula (2.1), as an equality between numerical classes. As we said
above, the computations will be closely related to the original computations of Green-Griﬃths [GG80]
(i.e. estimating Euler characteristics by integrals of polynomials on adequate simplexes). This leads
to the second purpose of this chapter: its computations will serve to introduce the main method of
Chapter 4, where we use once more these integrals on simplexes, but this time substituting the use of
asymptotic Riemann-Roch with the generalized algebraic Morse inequalities.
In this chapter,
k is a ﬁeld of arbitrary characteristic, not necessarily algebraically closed.

Lemma 2.2.1. Let X be a variety of dimension n ∈N. Let E →X be a vector bundle. Then there

2.3.1. Notation. We introduce the following conventions.
(1) For all n, we let voln denote the n-dimensional euclidean volume measure.
(2) Let m ∈N. A m-dimensional simplex ∆is a metric space isomorphic to the convex envelop of
m+1 points in Rm, such that any p of them generate an aﬃne (p−1)-space. We will sometimes
write ∆
○⊂Rm to emphasize the fact that ∆has non-empty interior in Rm (or equivalently,
that dim∆= m) and to oppose this situation to the case of a (m −1)-dimensional simplex
included in Rm.
(3) For any m-dimensional simplex ∆
○⊂Rm, the uniform probability measure of ∆is the measure

and if Ψ ∈GL(Rm) is such that ∆2 = Ψ(∆1), then Ψ sends the uniform measure of ∆1 on
the uniform measure of ∆2.

## Page 43


CHAPTER 3
Truncated intersection theory and Morse inequalities
This chapter presents the core result of this text: a generalized version of the algebraic Morse
inequalities, formulated in terms of a combinatorial data coming from a stratiﬁcation on a variety.
After some deﬁnition and general facts, we will prove this result in Section 3.3.
In this chapter,
k is a ﬁeld of arbitrary characteristic. Except in Section 3.4, we do not require it
to be algebraically closed.
3.1. Stratiﬁcations
3.1.1. Basic deﬁnitions. It is time to introduce the concept on which all the rest of the text will
be based: a basic notion of stratiﬁcation on a given variety. Our stratiﬁcations will be constructed
inductively, using the following two deﬁnitions.
Definition 3.1.1. Let Xn (resp. Xn−1) be a normal scheme of pure dimension n (resp. n −1).
A morphism Xn−1
f
Ð→Xn is called a stratum on Xn, if it factors through a reduced Weil divisor
Dn ⊂Xn, so that f ∶Xn−1 Ð→Dn is a proper birational morphism. In particular, f associates pairwise
the irreducible components of Xn−1 and of Dn.
Definition 3.1.2. A stratiﬁcation Σ = (X●,f●,q) on a reduced scheme X of pure dimension n, is
the data of a proper birational morphism q ∶Xn →X with Xn normal, and a sequence of strata of
maximal length on X′, of the form
X0
f0
Ð→X1
f1
Ð→...
fn−1
Ð→Xn
q
Ð→X.
Remark 3.1.3.
(a) In Deﬁnition 3.1.2, all the maps fi and q are part of the data. We will
sometimes omit q when introducing a given stratiﬁcation, and write e.g. ”Let Σ ∶= (X●,f●)
be a stratiﬁcation on X”.
(b) If we are given a stratiﬁcation Σ ∶= (X●,f●) on a reduced scheme X, each scheme Xi comes
with a natural map qi ∶Xi Ð→X, obtained by composition.
The following deﬁnition introduces a natural notion of trivialization of a vector bundle on a strat-
iﬁcation.
Definition 3.1.4. Let X be a reduced scheme of pure dimension n, and let E Ð→X be a vector
bundle of rank r. Consider a stratiﬁcation Σ ∶= (X●,f●) . For each i ∈J0,nK, let qi ∶Xi Ð→X be the
naturally induced map, and let Ui ∶= Xi ∖fi−1(Xi−1).
(a) A trivialization e of E over Σ, is the data, for each i ∈J0,nK, of a trivialization (ei
j)1≤j≤r of
q∗
i E on the dense open subset Ui ⊂Xi.
(b) A trivialized stratiﬁcation Σ = (Σ,e) for E is the data of a stratiﬁcation Σ of X and of a
trivialization e of E over Σ.
We say that Σ is adapted to E if there exists a trivialization of E over Σ.
The main case we will have in mind is the situation of a line bundle, where rk(E) = 1. It will also
be quite important to be able to deal with Q-line bundles. This will be permitted by the following
deﬁnition.
Definition 3.1.5. Let N be a Q-line bundle on X, and let Σ be a stratiﬁcation of X. A trivial-
ization of N on Σ is the data of a pair (d,e), where

It is essentially obvious that any vector bundle on a reduced scheme admits an adapted stratiﬁca-
tion. This is the content of the following proposition.
Proposition 3.1.7. Let E be a vector bundle (resp. a Q-line bundle) on a reduced pure dimensional
scheme X. Then E admits an adapted stratiﬁcation.
Proof. It suﬃces to deal with the case of a vector bundle.
Let us proceed by induction on
n = dim X. Let ﬁrst q ∶Xn →X be the normalization, and let Un ⊂X be an open dense subset,
above which q∗E admits a trivialization (en

applying the induction hypothesis to the vector bundle f ∗

□
The following deﬁnition introduces the combinatorial data that will be crucial in the statement of
our Morse inequalities. Basically, if we are given a stratiﬁcation Σ on a scheme X, it is possible to
build a tree by taking a node for every component of the strata, and connecting them in the obvious
manner.
Definition 3.1.8. Let Σ = (X●,f●) be a stratiﬁcation on a reduced scheme X of pure dimension
n. The associated tree to Σ is the graph deﬁned as follows:
(a) the nodes are indexed by the irreducible components of all the Xi;
(b) the root is the node corresponding to Xn.
(c) if ν is such a node, indexed by an irreducible component V ⊆Xi, then the children of ν are
indexed by the irreducible components W of Xi−1 that satisfy fi−1(W) ⊆V .
Remark 3.1.9. Let T be associated to a stratiﬁcation Σ, as in Deﬁnition 3.1.8. We see right away
that the following properties are satisﬁed:
(a) The leaves are in bijection with the points of X0.
(b) to each node ν of T is associated an irreducible variety Vν;
(c) to each arrow ν Ð→µ in T is associated a morphism fνµ ∶Vν Ð→Vµ;
(d) if Cµ is the set of children of µ, the natural map
cµ ∶⊔
ν∈Cµ
Vν Ð→Vµ
is a stratum of Vµ accordingly to Deﬁnition 3.1.1;

Proposition 3.1.12. Let E,F Ð→X be vector bundles (or Q-line bundles) on a reduced scheme
X, and let Σ be a stratiﬁcation adapted to E. There exists a stratiﬁcation Σ′, reﬁning Σ, and adapted

3. TRUNCATED INTERSECTION THEORY AND MORSE INEQUALITIES
3.2. Truncated ﬁrst Chern classes
The data of a trivialized stratiﬁcation with respect to a Q-bundle L contains all the information
necessary to compute the top intersection number ∫X c1(L)dimX. The purpose of this section is to
introduce truncated versions of these numbers. They will later play the same role in our algebraic
Morse inequalities, than the truncated integrals in Demailly’s holomorphic Morse inequalities.
To ease a bit the future proofs by induction, we will actually introduce a minimal subset of
intersection theory, that will allow us to give a recursive deﬁnition of these numbers.
3.2.1. Cycle groups and stratiﬁcations. Deﬁnition of the truncated Chern classes. Our next def-
inition introduces a cycle group associated to a given stratiﬁcation on X. We choose to use rational
coeﬃcients for our cycles: this will be well suited to prove Morse inequalities for Q-line bundles.
Definition 3.2.1. Let X be a reduced scheme of pure dimension n, and let Σ = (X●,f●) be a
stratiﬁcation of X. For each k ∈J0,nK, the k-th cycle group of Σ is the ﬁnite dimensional Q-vector
space
ZΣ

3.2.3. Finite morphisms and stratiﬁcations. Let p ∶X′ →X be a ﬁnite morphism between two
varieties. In this section, we explain how to produce a stratiﬁcation on X′, starting from a stratiﬁcation
on X, and we compare the truncated Chern classes obtained in this way. The straightforward idea is
to consider the ﬁber products and to take the adequate normalizations. Let us give some details.
3.2.3.1. Basic construction. Let X be a normal variety of dimension n, and let f ∶Y →X be a
stratum on X, where Y is a normal scheme of pure dimension n −1. Let D be the schematic image of
f in X: this is a Weil divisor in X, such that the corestriction f ∶Y →D is proper birational.
Consider now a ﬁnite morphism p ∶X′ →X, where X′ is a normal variety as well. The scheme
Y ×X X′ has pure dimension n −1. Denote by Y ′ the scheme obtained by taking the disjoint union of
the normalizations of all irreducible components of Y ×X X′: this is again a purely dimensional normal
scheme of dimension n −1. There is a naturally induced morphism f ′ ∶Y ′ →X′.
Now, D′ ∶= p−1(D) is also a Weil divisor in X′. For each component D′

3.3. Morse inequalities
In this section, X will be a proper variety of dimension n. In this situation, for any coherent sheaf
F on X, the cohomology groups

3.3.1. Truncated Euler characteristics. In essence, the algebraic Morse inequalities are a means to
estimate the asymptotic behaviour of truncated Euler characteristics, which are deﬁned as follows.
Definition 3.3.2. Let F be a coherent sheaf on X, and let l ∈J0,nK be an integer. The l-th
truncated Euler characteristic of F is the integer
χ[l](X,F) =
l
∑
j=0
(−1)j+lhj(X,F).
Note that in this deﬁnition, the number hl(X,F) comes with a positive sign. A very easy but
nonetheless crucial remark is that these numbers satisfy a subadditivity property with respect to short
exact sequences, that generalizes the additivity property of the usual Euler characteristic.
Lemma 3.3.3. Consider the following exact sequence of coherent sheaves on X:
0 Ð→F Ð→E Ð→G Ð→0
Then, for all integer l, one has the inequalities:
χ[l](X,E) ≤χ[l](X,F) + χ[l](X,G).
and
χ[l](X,F) ≤χ[l](X,E) + χ[l−1](X,G)
Proof. Write down the long exact sequence in cohomology, and truncate it after the term
Hl(X,G): for some ﬁnite dimensional vector space Z, we get a resolution
... Ð→Hl−1(X,G) Ð→Hl(X,F) Ð→Hl(X,E) Ð→Hl(X,G) Ð→Z Ð→0.
The result then follows from writing dim Z ≥0, and using that the Euler characteristic of the exact
sequence above equals
0 = dim Z −χ[l](X,G) + χ[l](X,E) −χ[l](X,F).
The second inequality follows from the same argument, writing this time:
... Ð→Hl−1(X,G) Ð→Hl(X,F) Ð→Hl(X,E) Ð→Z Ð→0.
□
We can iterate the lemma above, and derive the following consequence for ﬁltered sheaves on X:

3.3.2. Statement of the Morse inequalities. We are now in position to state and prove the following
algebraic version of the Morse inequalities.
Theorem 3.3.5 (Morse inequalities). Let X be a proper variety of dimension n. Let L be a Q-line
bundle on X, and let Σ ∶= (Σ, 1
de) be a trivialized stratiﬁcation adapted to L. Let M be another line
bundle on X. Then, for each integer i, and for any m such that L⊗m is a line bundle, we have

[≤i]) mn
n! + O(mn−1)
(ii) (Weak Morse inequalities)
hi(X,M ⊗L⊗m) ≤(−1)i (deg c1(L,Σ)n
[i]) mn
n! + O(mn−1)
(iii) (Asymptotic Riemann-Roch formula)
χ(X,M ⊗L⊗m) = (deg c1(L,Σ)n
[≤n]) mn
n! + O(mn−1)
Remark 3.3.6. We included this truncated version of the asymptotic Riemann-Roch formula
above for completeness, but of course this statement is none other than the usual, untruncated version
(see e.g. Proposition 3.2.10).
Before starting the proof, let us make the following simplifying remark.
Lemma 3.3.7. Theorem 3.3.5 is implied by the same statement, where L is assumed to be a line
bundle.
Proof. Let f ∈N≥1 be the smallest positive integer such that L⊗f is a standard line bundle.
Now, assume that the results holds for L⊗f, with the trivialized stratiﬁcation Σ′ ∶= (Σ, 1
de⊗f) (see

3.3.3. Proof in the case where L is a line bundle. As usual, the weak Morse inequalities follow
from the strong ones, remarking that hi = χ[i] + χ[i−1]. Also, the asymptotic Riemann-Roch formula
can be obtained from the strong Morse inequalities, using χ = (−1)nχ[n] = (−1)n+1χ[n+1]. Thus, it
suﬃces to prove the ﬁrst point.
Remark 3.3.8. Let us make some comments on the strategy. To simplify the exposition, assume
that X is normal.
(1) The proof will be induction on dim X, the base case being essentially trivial. The general
induction argument will be a standard telescopic argument of the sort that can be used to
prove the asymptotic Riemann-Roch theorem (see e.g. the presentation of [Deb01, Section
1.2]). The idea is to write
L = O(A −B)
where A −B is the divisor of poles and zeros of a trivialization e induced by e on X. Then,
we can write short exact sequences such as in Lemma 3.3.3 to get the relevant inequality (see
(3.13) below). There are however two main technical issues.
(2) The ﬁrst problem is that even if A−B is a Cartier divisor, its components are a priori merely
Weil divisors. This leads to problems when trying to estimate the cohomology of OA ⊗L⊗m,
for example (our plan is to proceed by exhibiting a ﬁltration on this sheaf, that works only if
the divisor has been decomposed in a sum of Cartier divisors; see Lemma 3.3.15 below). For
this reason, we might have to perform blowing-ups on X.
(3) The second issue comes from the fact that our data is a priori adapted to L⊗d and not L. We
resolve this discrepancy by taking a cyclic cover on X.
(4) Because of the clean-up steps required by the two points above, we naturally end up with
diagrams of the form
X′
...
Xn−1
X
p
fn−2
fn−1
where we want to prove the Morse inequalities for p∗L on X′, with our stratiﬁcation nonethe-
less still deﬁned on X. This relative situation forces us to use a slightly more general induction
step, that will be given by Proposition 3.3.9.
We are now ready to give the proof of Theorem 3.3.5 when L is a line bundle. It will be complete
with the following result.
Proposition 3.3.9. Under the hypotheses of Theorem 3.3.5, assume moreover that L is a line
bundle. Let p ∶X′ Ð→X be a proper dominant, generically ﬁnite morphism of degree δ ∈N≥1, where

Remark 3.3.16. Before ending this section, we want to emphasize the importance of stating
Proposition 3.3.9 without necessarily assuming that X is normal, since in our induction steps, the
divisorial subschemes Dj ⊂X may well be non-normal.
However, it is crucial that X is normal to be able to deﬁne the multiplicities of a meromorphic
section, hence the necessity of Step 0 at the beginning of the proof.

## Page 63


CHAPTER 4
Morse inequalities and weighted symmetric products
The main theorem of this chapter is an asymptotic estimate on the growth of quantities of the form
χ[i](X,N ⊗m ⊗Sm (E)), where E is a weighted direct sum of line bundles, and N is an arbitrary Q-line
bundle; it will be the central step in our proof of existence of jet diﬀerentials on varieties of general
type. The main strategy will be to proceed as in the proof of Proposition 2.3.1, but substituting the
use of the asymptotic Riemann-Roch theorem with the algebraic Morse inequalities of the previous
chapter.
In this chapter, we do not assume that
k is algebraically closed.
4.1. Notation and statement of the result
We refer to Section 2.3.1 for some basic notation about simplexes.
Definition 4.1.1. For all a = (a1,... ,ar) ∈Nr
≥1, we deﬁne the (r −1)-dimensional simplex

Remark 4.1.10. Let us a make a few comments about the proof of the theorem.
(1) We will ﬁrst make a reduction step to pass from the situation where the Li are standard line
bundles. It will suﬃce to perform Bloch-Gieseker coverings, and to imitate the discussion of
Section 3.2.3; characteristic p phenomena will not make the process especially complicated.
(2) After this ﬁrst reduction step, the idea is to proceed as in the proof of Proposition 2.3.1 (see
Section 2.3.5), but substituting the use of Riemann-Roch theorem with the algebraic Morse
inequalities of Theorem 3.3.5.
(3) The symmetric product Sm(L(a1)
1
⊕...⊕L(ar)
r
) will decompose as a direct sum of line bundles,
each coming with a natural trivialization of Σ. One ﬁrst diﬃculty is that instead of dealing
with a unique line bundle L for which we want to estimate χ[i](X,L⊗m), each line bundle in
the direct sum is a product of powers of some of the Li with total (weighted) degree m. This
forces us to use a variant of Theorem 3.3.5, adapted to this situation where we deal with a
product of ﬁxed line bundles (see Proposition 4.3.4).
(4) Now, we want to apply Proposition 4.3.4 to each of the line bundles in the expansion of the
symmetric product, and sum the inequalities. We need to make the right hand side converge
to an integral, but we are faced with another problem, as the Morse inequalities may assume
quite diﬀerent forms from one line bundle to another. To circumvent this problem, we will
need to better estimate the right hand sides that can appear this way: actually, we will ﬁrst
give an upper bound on the χ[i](X,L⊗l1
1
⊗...⊗L⊗lr
r
), holding for any tuple (l1,... ,lr) allowed
to vary in a small angular sector of Nr (see Proposition 4.4.4). Finally, we will sum over a
covering of Nr by arbitrarily small angular sectors: the previous estimates will then converge
nicely to the integral appearing in Theorem 4.1.9 (see Section 4.5).
4.2. Reduction step. Bloch-Gieseker coverings
The goal of this section is to get back to the case where the Q-line bundles have been replaced
with standard line bundles.
This will be achieved in Proposition 4.2.6, after some clean-up using
Bloch-Gieseker coverings.

at Section 3.2.3 on stratiﬁcations induced on ﬁnite morphisms, and maybe to read again the notation
of Setup 4.1.2.
The following result is the well-known statement provided by the Bloch-Gieseker covering con-
struction.

CHAPTER 5
Existence of jet diﬀerentials on varieties of general type
In this chapter, we prove Demailly’s existence theorem for Hasse-Schmidt jet diﬀerentials on a
smooth projective variety of general type.
In this section, we assume that
k is a an arbitrary algebraically closed ﬁeld.
The main result is the following:
Theorem 5.0.1 ([Dem11], for
k = C). Let X be a smooth projective variety over the ﬁeld
k, and
let A be an ample divisor on X. Assume that X is of general type. Then, for a suﬃciently large ﬁxed
k ≫1, the OX-algebra EHS
k,●ΩX is big. In other words, there exists a constant Ck > 0 such that for
m ≫1 suﬃciently divisible, we have
h0(X,EHS
k,mΩX) ≥Ckmn+kn−1
This implies that for m ≫k ≫1, we also have
H0(X,EHS
k,mΩX ⊗OX(−A)) ≠0.
Recall that we say that a smooth projective variety X is of general type if its canonical bundle is
big, i.e. satisﬁes
KX ∼Q A + E
where A and E are respectively ample and eﬀective Q-Cartier divisors.

A.1.2. Asymptotic Riemann-Roch theorem. Let X be any proper variety over
k, and let n =
dimX. It follows from [Ful98, Section 18.3] that we can deﬁne the Todd class of X as a class Td(X) ∈
A∗(X)Q
Td(X) = [X] + terms of dimension < n.
If E →X is any vector bundle, we then have the following Riemann-Roch theorem (see [Ful98,
Corollary 18.3.1 (a)]);
χ(X,E) = deg (ch(E) ∩Td(X))n .
In particular, if L is a line bundle on X, then we have the asymptotic expansion
χ(X,L⊗m) = (deg c1(L) ∩[X]) mn
n! + O(mn−2),
where the O(mn−2) term can actually be expanded as a polynomial in m with coeﬃcients depending
only on Td(X) and c1(L).
A.2. Cyclic covers and modiﬁcations
We gather here a few results that are very classical in the complex setting (or are essentially trivial
if we admit the existence of strong resolutions of singularities), but for which we could not ﬁnd a
satisfactory reference in the general case. Note that in most cases, the complex proof work without
much change in the general setting.
Lemma A.2.1. Let X be a variety, endowed with a line bundle L. Let d ∈N∗. Let U ⊂X be an
open subset on which L⊗d is trivial, and let e ∈Γ(U,L⊗d) be a trivializing section. Then there exists a
ﬁnite dominant morphism p ∶X′ →X, where X′ is a variety, such that p∗L is trivial on U ′ ∶= p−1(U),
with a trivializing section e′ such that (e′)⊗d = p∗e.
Proof. Let e be a trivializing section of L⊗d on U. Introduce the completion of L⊗d at inﬁnity
as P = P(L⊗d ⊕OX), endowed with its projection q ∶P Ð→X. The total space of L⊗d∣U −{0} embeds

As a special case, we get the following classical result.
Lemma A.2.3. Let X be a variety, endowed with a line bundle L. Let d ∈N∗be such that L⊗d
is eﬀective, and let D be an eﬀective associated Cartier divisor. Then there exists a ﬁnite dominant
morphism p ∶X′ →X, where X′ is a variety, such that p∗D = dD′ for some eﬀective Cartier divisor
D′ on X.

and e being the global section of L⊗d giving D. In this case, we see from the proof that e′ comes from
a regular section of p∗L, giving a divisor D′ as intended.
□

Proposition A.2.5. Let X be a projective variety, and let D be an eﬀective Cartier divisor on

## Page 101


APPENDIX C
Weighted projectivized bundles and stacks
In this appendix, we present some general facts about the algebraic stacks that appear in this
text as weighted projective spaces: the material of this section is classical, and its main purpose is to
help the reader ﬁx their ideas without needing to revisit the whole theory. In particular, we will put
some emphasis on the positive characteristic phenomena that can happen in this context: one of the
main consequences is that the considered stacks will not be Deligne-Mumford in general, but merely
algebraic stacks admitting an fppf atlas.
Our main reference for this section will be the Stacks project and more particularly [Sta25, Part
0ELS].
We ﬁx a ﬁeld
k. All schemes will be of ﬁnite type above
k, and all direct products will be taken
above Spec
k. Let p = char(k). The following discussion will be relevant for any value of p, but several
remarks will become essentially empty if p = 0.
C.1. Torsors in the fppf topology
Algebraic stacks can be seen locally as classifying objects for torsors above schemes. To deﬁne
torsors, we need to specify a Grothendieck topology: the fppf topology will the best suited for our
purposes. We will not recall the axioms of a site or a Grothendieck topology, for which we refer to
[Sta25, Deﬁnition 00VH]. The following deﬁnition will be suﬃcient for our discussion.
Definition C.1.1. Let X be a scheme of ﬁnite type over Spec
k. A fppf 1 covering of X is the
data of a collection of morphisms of
k-schemes {fi ∶Yi →X}i∈I where each fi is ﬂat, locally of ﬁnite
presentation, and such that X = ⋃i∈I fi(Yi).
Remark C.1.2. Using this terminology, we can now deﬁne a fppf local version for any property
(P) of
k-schemes that is invariant under base change: a given scheme X satisﬁes this property locally
for the fppf topology if there exists a covering {fi ∶Yi →X} as above such that the union Y = ⊔i Yi
satisﬁes (P), possibly after pulling-back the relevant data from X to Y .
A fppf torsor (or simply torsor, for short) can in particular be deﬁned as a principal bundle that
is locally trivial for the fppf topology:
Definition C.1.3. Let G be a group scheme of ﬁnite type above
k. Let X be a
k-scheme of ﬁnite
type. An G-torsor above X for the fppf topology is the data of
(a) a surjective morphism of
k-schemes π ∶P →X;

where a line bundle L →X is sent to the Gm-torsor L −{0} ∶= SpecX (⊕m∈Z L⊗m), endowed with its

Remark C.1.5. In particular, Gm-torsors are locally trivial in the Zariski topology as well. This
property makes Gm a special group in the sense of Serre (cf. [Ser58])
Proof. This is essentially a special case of [Sta25, Theorem 03P8]. Let us simply recall how to
construct a quasi-inverse for the functor described in the proposition, using fppf descent theory. Let
P →X be a Gm-torsor that is trivialized on some fppf covering {fi ∶Yi →X}i∈I. Now, for each i ∈I,
we have an isomorphism
Yi ×fi P ≅Yi × Spec
k[λ,λ−1].
Denoting Pi ∶= Yi×fi P and letting πi ∶Pi →Yi be the canonical projection, let Li be the OY -submodule
of (πi)∗OPi, generated by the global coordinate λ. Each Li is a trivial line bundle above Yi. Now,
we check that the Li admit a descent data, induced by the natural descent data for the family (Pi)i∈I
coming from P. Any descent data for coherent sheaves in the fppf topology is eﬀective (this is even
true for fpqc coverings: see [Sta25, Proposition 023T]); this shows that there exists a coherent sheaf
L on X pulling back to the Li. Finally, since Zariski local freeness of ﬁnite modules can be checked in
the fppf topology (see [Sta25, Lemma 05B1]), the freeness of the Li implies that L is Zariski locally
free as well. Finally, an isomorphism between L −{0} and P can be constructed ﬁrst on the schemes
Yi, and then descended to X, using again a natural descent data.
□

C.2.4. The Deligne-Mumford locus. The Deligne-Mumford locus of P ∶= P(a0,a1,... ,an) is the
largest open substack P○that is Deligne-Mumford, i.e. admits a surjective ´etale morphism U →P○
from some scheme U. This locus can be described as the complement of the weighted projective stack
where we have kept only the ai divisible by p. In particular, P itself is Deligne-Mumford if p = 0.

this gives the result.

back to Proposition C.2.13.
